Specify the `dtype` and\n        `na_value` keywords to customize the conversion.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        dtype : dtype, default object\n            The numpy dtype to convert to.\n        copy : bool, default False\n            Whether to ensure that the returned value is a not a view on\n            the array. includes handling object arrays containing imaginary\n    # numbers and complex types with non-native byteorder\n    else:\n        x = um.multiply(x, um.conjugate(x), out=x).real\n\n    ret = umr_sum(x, axis, dtype, out, keepdims=keepdims, where=where)\n\n    # Compute degrees of freedom and make sure it is not negative.\n    rcount = um.maximum(rcount - ddof, 0)\n\n    # divide by degrees of freedom\n    if isinstance(ret, mu.ndarray):\n        ret = um.true_divide(\n                ret, rcount, out=ret, casting='unsafe', subok=False)\n    elif hasattr(ret, 'dtype'):\n        ret = ret.dtype.type(ret / rcount)\n    else:\n        ret = ret / rcount\n\n    return ret\n\ndef _std(a, axis=None, dtype=None, out=None, ddof=0, keepdims=False, *,\n         where=True, mean=None):\n    ret = _var(a, axis=axis, dtype=dtype, out=out, ddof=ddof,\n               keepdims=keepdims, where=where, mean=mean)\n\n    if isinstance(ret, mu.ndarray):\n        ret = um.sqrt(ret, out=ret)\n    elif hasattr(ret, 'dtype'):\n        ret = ret.dtype.type(um.sqrt(ret))\n    else:\n        ret = um.sqrt(ret)\n\n    return ret\n\ndef _ptp(a, axis=None, out=None, keepdims=False):\n    return um.subtract(\n        umr_maximum(a, axis, None, out, keepdims),\n        umr_minimum(a, axis, None, None, keepdims),\n        out\n    )\n\ndef _dump(self, file, protocol=2):\n    if hasattr(file, 'write'):\n        ctx = nullcontext(file)\n    else:\n        ctx = open(os.fspath(file), \"wb\")\n    with ctx as f:\n        pickle.dump(self, f, protocol=protocol)\n\ndef _dumps(self, protocol=2):\n    return pickle.dumps(self, protocol=protocol)\n\ndef _bitwise_count(a, out=None, *, where=True, casting='same_kind',\n          order='K', dtype=None, subok=True):\n    return umr_bitwise_count(a, out, where=where, casting=casting,\n            order=order, dtype=dtype, subok=subok)\n","numLines":253,"startLine":1,"totalLines":253}}}

Mean and\\n755:     standard deviation are then stored to be used on later data using\\n756:     :meth:`transform`.\\n757: \\n758:     Standardization of a dataset is a common requirement for many\\n759:     machine learning estimators: they might behave badly if the\\n760:     individual features do not more or less look like standard normally\\n761:     distributed data (e.g. Gaussian with 0 mean and unit variance).\\n762: \\n763:     For instance many elements used in the objective function of\\n764:     a learning algorithm (such as the RBF kernel of Support Vector\\n765:     Machines or the L1 and L2 regularizers of linear models) assume that\\n766:     all features are centered around 0 and have variance in the same\\n767:     order. If a feature has a variance that is orders of magnitude larger\\n768:     than others, it might dominate the objective function and make the\\n769:     estimator unable to learn from other features correctly as expected.\\n770: \\n771:     `StandardScaler` is sensitive to outliers, and the features may scale\\n772:     differently from each other in the presence of outliers. For an example\\n773:     visualization, refer to :ref:`Compare StandardScaler with other scalers\\n774:     <plot_all_scaling_standard_scaler_section>`.\\n775: \\n776:     This scaler can also be applied to sparse CSR or CSC matrices by passing\\n777:     `with_mean=False` to avoid breaking the sparsity structure of the data.\\n778: \\n779:     Read more in the :ref:`User Guide <preprocessing_scaler>`.\\n780: \\n781:     Parameters\\n782:     ----------\\n783:     copy : bool, default=True\\n784:         If False, try to avoid a copy and do inplace scaling instead.\\n785:         This is not guaranteed to always work inplace; e.g. if the data is\\n786:         not a NumPy array or scipy.sparse CSR matrix, a copy may still be\\n787:         returned.\\n788: \\n789:     with_mean : bool, default=True\\n790:         If True, center the data before scaling.\\n791:         This does not work (and will raise an exception) when attempted on\\n792:         sparse matrices, because centering them entails building a dense\\n793:         matrix which in common use cases is likely to be too large to fit in\\n794:         memory.\\n795: \\n796:     with_std : bool, default=True\\n797:         If True, scale the data to unit variance (or equivalently,\\n798:         unit standard deviation).\\n799: \\n800:     Attributes\\n801:     ----------\\n802:     scale_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\\n803:         Per feature relative scaling of the data to achieve zero mean and unit\\n804:         variance. Generally this is calculated using `np.sqrt(var_)`. If a\\n805:         variance is zero, we can't achieve unit variance, and the data is left\\n806:         as-is, giving a scaling factor of 1. `scale_` is equal to `None`\\n807:         when `with_std=False`.\\n808: \\n809:         .. versionadded:: 0.17\\n810:            *scale_*\\n811: \\n812:     mean_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\\n813:         The mean value for each feature in the training set.\\n814:         Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and ``with_std=False``.\\n815: \\n816:     var_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\\n817:         The variance for each feature in the training set. Used to compute\\n818:         `scale_`. Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and\\n819:         ``with_std=False``.\\n820: \\n821:     n_features_in_ : int\\n822:         Number of features seen during :term:`fit`.\\n823: \\n824:         .. versionadded:: 0.24\\n825: \\n826:     feature_names_in_ : ndarray of shape (`n_features_in_`,)\\n827:         Names of features seen during :term:`fit`. Defined only when `X`\\n828:         has feature names that are all strings.\\n829: \\n830:         .. versionadded:: 1.0\\n831: \\n832:     n_samples_seen_ : int or ndarray of shape (n_features,)\\n833:         The number of samples processed by the estimator for each feature.\\n834:         If there are no missing samples, the ``n_samples_seen`` will be an\\n835:         integer, otherwise it will be an array of dtype int. If\\n836:         `sample_weights` are used it will be a float (if no missing data)\\n837:         or an array of dtype float that sums the weights seen so far.\\n838:         Will be reset on new calls to fit, but increments across\\n839:         ``partial_fit`` calls.\\n840: \\n841:     See Also\\n842:     --------\\n843:     scale : Equivalent function without the estimator API.\\n844: \\n845:     :class:`~sklearn.decomposition.PCA` : Further removes the linear\\n846:         correlation across features with 'whiten=True'.\\n847: \\n848:     Notes\\n849:     -----\\n850:     NaNs are treated as missing values: disregarded in fit, and maintained in\\n851:     transform.\\n852: \\n853:     We use a biased estimator for the standard deviation, equivalent to\\n854:     `numpy.std(x, ddof=0)`. Note that the choice of `ddof` is unlikely to\\n855:     affect model performance.\\n856: \\n857:     Examples\\n858:     --------\\n859:     >>> from sklearn.preprocessing import class StandardScaler(OneToOneFeatureMixin, TransformerMixin, BaseEstimator):\n741:     \"\"\"Standardize features by removing the mean and scaling to unit variance.\n742: \n743:     The standard score of a sample `x` is calculated as:\n744: \n745:     .. code-block:: text\n746: \n747:         z = (x - u) / s\n748: \n749:     where `u` is the mean of the training samples or zero if `with_mean=False`,\n750:     and `s` is the standard deviation of the training samples or one if\n751:     `with_std=False`.\n752: \n753:     Centering and scaling happen independently on each feature by computing\n754:     the relevant statistics on the samples in the training set. Mean and\n755:     standard deviation are then stored to be used on later data using\n756:     :meth:`transform`.\n757: \n758:     Standardization of a dataset is a common requirement for many\n759:     machine learning estimators: they might behave badly if the\n760:     individual features do not more or less look like standard normally\n761:     distributed data (e.g. Gaussian with 0 mean and unit variance).\n762: \n763:     For instance many elements used in the objective function of\n764:     a learning algorithm (such as the RBF kernel of Support Vector\n765:     Machines or the L1 and L2 regularizers of linear models) assume that\n766:     all features are centered around 0 and have variance in the same\n767:     order. If a feature has a variance that is orders of magnitude larger\n768:     than others, it might dominate the objective function and make the\n769:     estimator unable to learn from other features correctly as expected.\n770: \n771:     `StandardScaler` is sensitive to outliers, and the features may scale\n772:     differently from each other in the presence of outliers. For an example\n773:     visualization, refer to :ref:`Compare StandardScaler with other scalers\n774:     <plot_all_scaling_standard_scaler_section>`.\n775: \n776:     This scaler can also be applied to sparse CSR or CSC matrices by passing\n777:     `with_mean=False` to avoid breaking the sparsity structure of the data.\n778: \n779:     Read more in the :ref:`User Guide <preprocessing_scaler>`.\n780: \n781:     Parameters\n782:     ----------\n783:     copy : bool, default=True\n784:         If False, try to avoid a copy and do inplace scaling instead.\n785:         This is not guaranteed to always work inplace; e.g. if the data is\n786:         not a NumPy array or scipy.sparse CSR matrix, a copy may still be\n787:         returned.\n788: \n789:     with_mean : bool, default=True\n790:         If True, center the data before scaling.\n791:         This does not work (and will raise an exception) when attempted on\n792:         sparse matrices, because centering them entails building a dense\n793:         matrix which in common use cases is likely to be too large to fit in\n794:         memory.\n795: \n796:     with_std : bool, default=True\n797:         If True, scale the data to unit variance (or equivalently,\n798:         unit standard deviation).\n799: \n800:     Attributes\n801:     ----------\n802:     scale_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n803:         Per feature relative scaling of the data to achieve zero mean and unit\n804:         variance. Generally this is calculated using `np.sqrt(var_)`. If a\n805:         variance is zero, we can't achieve unit variance, and the data is left\n806:         as-is, giving a scaling factor of 1. `scale_` is equal to `None`\n807:         when `with_std=False`.\n808: \n809:         .. versionadded:: 0.17\n810:            *scale_*\n811: \n812:     mean_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n813:         The mean value for each feature in the training set.\n814:         Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and ``with_std=False``.\n815: \n816:     var_ : ndarray of shape (n_features,) or None\n817:         The variance for each feature in the training set. Used to compute\n818:         `scale_`. Equal to ``None`` when ``with_mean=False`` and\n819:         ``with_std=False``.\n820: \n821:     n_features_in_ : int\n822:         Number of features seen during :term:`fit`.\n823: \n824:         .. versionadded:: 0.24\n825: \n826:     feature_names_in_ : ndarray of shape (`n_features_in_`,)\n827:         Names of features seen during :term:`fit`. Defined only when `X`\n828:         has feature names that are all strings.\n829: \n830:         .. versionadded:: 1.0\n831: \n832:     n_samples_seen_ : int or ndarray of shape (n_features,)\n833:         The number of samples processed by the estimator for each feature.\n834:         If there are no missing samples, the ``n_samples_seen`` will be an\n835:         integer, otherwise it will be an array of dtype int. If\n836:         `sample_weights` are used it will be a float (if no missing data)\n837:         or an array of dtype float that sums the weights seen so far.\n838:         Will be reset on new calls to fit, but increments across\n839:         ``partial_fit`` calls.\n840: \n841:     See Also\n842:     --------\n843:     scale : Equivalent function without the estimator API.\n844: \n845:     :class:`~sklearn.decomposition.PCA` : Further removes the linear\n846:         correlation across features with 'whiten=True'.\n847: \n848:     Notes\n849:     -----\n850:     NaNs are treated as missing values: disregarded in fit, and maintained in\n851:     transform.\n852: \n853:     We use a biased estimator for the standard deviation, equivalent to\n854:     `numpy.std(x, ddof=0)`. Note that the choice of `ddof` is unlikely to\n855:     affect model performance.\n856: \n857:     Examples\n858:     --------\n859:     >>> from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\n860:     >>> data = [[0, 0], [0, 0], [1, 1], [1, 1]]\n861:     >>> scaler = StandardScaler()\n862:     >>> print(scaler.fit(data))\n863:     StandardScaler()\n864:     >>> print(scaler.mean_)\n865:     [0.5 0.5]\n866:     >>> print(scaler.transform(data))\n867:     [[-1. -1.]\n868:      [-1. -1.]\n869:      [ 1.  1.]\n870:      [ 1.  1.]]\n871:     >>> print(scaler.transform([[2, 2]]))\n872:     [[3. 3.]]\n873:     \"\"\"\n874: \n875:     _parameter_constraints: dict = {\n876:         \"copy\": [\"boolean\"],\n877:         \"with_mean\": [\"boolean\"],\n878:         \"with_std\": [\"boolean\"],\n879:     }\n880: \n881:     def __init__(self, *, copy=True, with_mean=True, with_std=True):\n882:         self.with_mean = with_mean\n883:         self.with_std = with_std\n884:         self.copy = copy\n885: \n886:     def _reset(self):\n887:         \"\"\"Reset internal data-dependent state of the scaler, if necessary.\n888: \n889:         __init__ parameters are not touched.\n890:         \"\"\"\n891:         # For extension dtypes that have a numpy equivalent\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype.numpy_dtype\n    else:\n        # Set to a safe default for numeric extension types\n        dtype_orig = np.float64 if pandas_requires_conversion else None\n```\n\n## Summary Table\n\n| Component | File | Line Range | Issue |\n|-----------|------|------------|-------|\n| **scikit-learn validation** | `sklearn/utils/validation.py` | 909-942 | `dtype_orig` set to `None` for extension dtypes, causing unpredictable conversion |\n| **pandas dtype inference** | `pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py` | 35-52 | Different behavior for arrays with/without NA values |\n| **pandas to_numpy** | `pandas/core/arrays/masked.py` | 512-606 | Conversion depends on NA presence |\n| **NumPy mean/var** | `numpy/_core/_methods.py` | 125-130, 158-160 | Internal int->float conversion happens after sklearn validation |\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":0,"cache_creation_input_tokens":313,"cache_read_input_tokens":83494,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":313,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard"},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"3b56e6d7-a793-4a50-912f-0abac12ea7e6","uuid":"eae0de49-7059-4ba6-81c9-664a71b19886"}

<-- This is problematic for extension dtypes\n```\n\nWhen `dtype_orig` is `None` and `pandas_requires_conversion` is `True`, the conversion at line 939-940 uses `None` as the target dtype:\n\n```python\nnew_dtype = dtype_orig if dtype is None else dtype  # new_dtype could be None\narray = array.astype(new_dtype)  # astype(None) behavior is unpredictable\n```\n\n**Line Range of Root Cause:** `sklearn/utils/validation.py:909-942`\n\n## Secondary Contributing Factors\n\n1. **Pandas `to_numpy_dtype_inference()`**: `/ccb_crossrepo/src/pandas/pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py:35-52`\n   - The inference logic differs based on whether NA values are present\n   - This creates inconsistent behavior between nullable integer arrays with and without NA values\n\n2. **NumPy mean/var dtype handling**: `/ccb_crossrepo/src/numpy/numpy/_core/_methods.py:125-130, 158-160`\n   - NumPy internally converts integers to float64 for mean/variance computation\n   - However, this happens late in the pipeline, after scikit-learn's validation\n\n## Impact\n\n1. **Inconsistent Results:** Nullable integer arrays with NA values convert to `float64` with `np.nan`, while those without NA may stay as integers or convert differently\n2. **Potential Errors:** When `dtype_orig` is `None` and the requested dtype is also `None`, the resulting array dtype is unpredictable\n3. **Silent Data Corruption:** Integer values may be silently converted to floats with potential precision loss for very large integers\n\n## Recommended Fix\n\nThe fix should be in `sklearn/utils/validation.py` around lines 909-942:\n\n```python\n# Suggested fix: Explicitly handle extension dtypes\nelif (_is_extension_array_dtype(array) or hasattr(array, \"iloc\")) and hasattr(\n    array, \"dtype\"\n):\n    # array is a pandas series\n    type_if_series = type(array)\n    pandas_requires_conversion = _pandas_dtype_needs_early_conversion(array.dtype)\n    if isinstance(array.dtype, np.dtype):\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype\n    elif hasattr(array.dtype, 'numpy_dtype'):\n        # For extension dtypes that have a numpy equivalent\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype.numpy_dtype\n    else:\n        # Set to a safe default for numeric extension types\n        dtype_orig = np.float64 if pandas_requires_conversion else None\n```\n\n## Summary Table\n\n| Component | File | Line Range | Issue |\n|-----------|------|------------|-------|\n| **scikit-learn validation** | `sklearn/utils/validation.py` | 909-942 | `dtype_orig` set to `None` for extension dtypes, causing unpredictable conversion |\n| **pandas dtype inference** | `pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py` | 35-52 | Different behavior for arrays with/without NA values |\n| **pandas to_numpy** | `pandas/core/arrays/masked.py` | 512-606 | Conversion depends on NA presence |\n| **NumPy mean/var** | `numpy/_core/_methods.py` | 125-130, 158-160 | Internal int->float conversion happens after sklearn validation |\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

NumPy's mean/var functions handle this internally, but the dtype mismatch can cause issues in intermediate computations\n\n## The Root Cause\n\n**Primary Root Cause:** `sklearn/utils/validation.py:894-896, 909-913, 935-940`\n\nThe root cause is in scikit-learn's `check_array()` function, specifically in how it handles the `dtype_orig` variable for pandas extension array dtypes:\n\n```python\n# Line 909-913\nelif (_is_extension_array_dtype(array) or hasattr(array, \"iloc\")) and hasattr(\n    array, \"dtype\"\n):\n    # array is a pandas series\n    # ...\n    if isinstance(array.dtype, np.dtype):\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype\n    else:\n        # Set to None to let array.astype work out the best dtype\n        dtype_orig = None  # <-- This is problematic for extension dtypes\n```\n\nWhen `dtype_orig` is `None` and `pandas_requires_conversion` is `True`, the conversion at line 939-940 uses `None` as the target dtype:\n\n```python\nnew_dtype = dtype_orig if dtype is None else dtype  # new_dtype could be None\narray = array.astype(new_dtype)  # astype(None) behavior is unpredictable\n```\n\n**Line Range of Root Cause:** `sklearn/utils/validation.py:909-942`\n\n## Secondary Contributing Factors\n\n1. **Pandas `to_numpy_dtype_inference()`**: `/ccb_crossrepo/src/pandas/pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py:35-52`\n   - The inference logic differs based on whether NA values are present\n   - This creates inconsistent behavior between nullable integer arrays with and without NA values\n\n2. **NumPy mean/var dtype handling**: `/ccb_crossrepo/src/numpy/numpy/_core/_methods.py:125-130, 158-160`\n   - NumPy internally converts integers to float64 for mean/variance computation\n   - However, this happens late in the pipeline, after scikit-learn's validation\n\n## Impact\n\n1. **Inconsistent Results:** Nullable integer arrays with NA values convert to `float64` with `np.nan`, while those without NA may stay as integers or convert differently\n2. **Potential Errors:** When `dtype_orig` is `None` and the requested dtype is also `None`, the resulting array dtype is unpredictable\n3. **Silent Data Corruption:** Integer values may be silently converted to floats with potential precision loss for very large integers\n\n## Recommended Fix\n\nThe fix should be in `sklearn/utils/validation.py` around lines 909-942:\n\n```python\n# Suggested fix: Explicitly handle extension dtypes\nelif (_is_extension_array_dtype(array) or hasattr(array, \"iloc\")) and hasattr(\n    array, \"dtype\"\n):\n    # array is a pandas series\n    type_if_series = type(array)\n    pandas_requires_conversion = _pandas_dtype_needs_early_conversion(array.dtype)\n    if isinstance(array.dtype, np.dtype):\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype\n    elif hasattr(array.dtype, 'numpy_dtype'):\n        # For extension dtypes that have a numpy equivalent\n        dtype_orig = array.dtype.numpy_dtype\n    else:\n        # Set to a safe default for numeric extension types\n        dtype_orig = np.float64 if pandas_requires_conversion else None\n```\n\n## Summary Table\n\n| Component | File | Line Range | Issue |\n|-----------|------|------------|-------|\n| **scikit-learn validation** | `sklearn/utils/validation.py` | 909-942 | `dtype_orig` set to `None` for extension dtypes, causing unpredictable conversion |\n| **pandas dtype inference** | `pandas/core/arrays/_utils.py` | 35-52 | Different behavior for arrays with/without NA values |\n| **pandas to_numpy** | `pandas/core/arrays/masked.py` | 512-606 | Conversion depends on NA presence |\n| **NumPy mean/var** | `numpy/_core/_methods.py` | 125-130, 158-160 | Internal int->float conversion happens after sklearn validation |\n","numLines":226,"startLine":1,"totalLines":226}}}
